========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I came in because I can’t really get this thought to stop. Like, even when I know I did the safety check on my cab — I’ll remember doing it, or at least I think I remember doing it — this thought just hits me that maybe I missed something. Like maybe there’s something wrong with the cab and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it.

And it’s not just a quick worry and then it goes away. It keeps coming back while I’m driving, and then I’m trying to focus on the road but in my head I’m going over it again and again, like, “Did I really check that? What if I skipped something?” It makes it hard to drive normally, and even when I’m trying to study it’s there too, like I can’t shut it up. It’s exhausting, honestly.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s kind of embarrassing because it starts as a normal safety check, like what I’m supposed to do before taking a ride, but then I can’t stop at just doing it once.

I’ll go around the cab and check the tires, then the lights, then the doors, then the meter, then the paperwork, and I’ll think, “Okay, that’s done.” But then as soon as I’m about to let someone in or start driving, I get this feeling like it didn’t count, or like maybe I only looked but didn’t really check properly. So I go around again. And sometimes I’ll take pictures of things, like to prove to myself I checked them, but then even the pictures don’t always help because I’ll think maybe the picture was from before or maybe I didn’t check the right angle or something.

And if it doesn’t feel right — like if I get interrupted, or I’m not completely sure in my head — I’ll restart the whole thing from the beginning. Tires, lights, doors, meter, paperwork, all over again. It can happen even when passengers are standing there waiting, which makes me feel terrible, and sometimes I’m already late but I still can’t just leave it. It’s like I need the check to feel “complete,” not just be complete.

Yeah, that’s what it feels like. Like I know the checks are supposed to prevent something bad, but I take it way past the normal amount. Before I can take a ride, I keep going around the car checking the tires, the lights, the doors, the meter, the paperwork — and then even after I’ve done all of that, I’ll think, “What if I missed one thing and someone gets hurt?”

So I do it again. And sometimes again. And if it doesn’t feel right in my head, like if I wasn’t paying attention perfectly or someone distracted me, I’ll start the whole check over from the beginning. I’ve even taken pictures to try to prove to myself I checked, but then I start doubting that too.

And it happens even when passengers are waiting there and I’m already late, which makes me feel awful, but I still feel like I can’t just drive off unless I’m sure. It’s like I’m trying to stop an accident or stop myself from being blamed for one, even if part of me knows I already checked.

And sometimes I’ll take pictures of things, like to prove to myself I checked them, but then even the pictures don’t always help because I’ll think maybe the picture was from before or maybe I didn’t check the right angle or something.\n\nAnd if it doesn’t feel right — like if I get interrupted, or I’m not completely sure in my head — I’ll restart the whole thing from the beginning. Tires, lights, doors, meter, paperwork, all over again. It can happen even when passengers are standing there waiting, which makes me feel terrible, and sometimes I’m already late but I still can’t just leave it. It’s like I need the check to feel “complete,” not just be complete.\n\nDoctor: Are these repeated checks you're doing intended to prevent something bad from happening, like an accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s what it feels like. Like I know the checks are supposed to prevent something bad, but I take it way past the normal amount. Before I can take a ride, I keep going around the car checking the tires, the lights, the doors, the meter, the paperwork — and then even after I’ve done all of that, I’ll think, “What if I missed one thing and someone gets hurt?”\n\nSo I do it again. And sometimes again. And if it doesn’t feel right in my head, like if I wasn’t paying attention perfectly or someone distracted me, I’ll start the whole check over from the beginning. I’ve even taken pictures to try to prove to myself I checked, but then I start doubting that too.\n\nAnd it happens even when passengers are waiting there and I’m already late, which makes me feel awful, but I still feel like I can’t just drive off unless I’m sure. It’s like I’m trying to stop an accident or stop myself from being blamed for one, even if part of me knows I already checked.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs there something specific that you fear might happen if you don't complete these checks perfectly?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 19 / taxi_driver / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because even when I know I did my safety check, this thought keeps hitting me that I missed something on the cab and somebody could get hurt or my boss will say I lied, and I can't make it shut up while I'm driving or trying to study.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… the main thing is that I’ll miss something on the cab and then somebody gets hurt because of it. Like maybe I didn’t really check a tire properly, or a light, or something with the doors, and then I drive off and something happens. Even if I remember doing the check, there’s this thought that comes in like, “But what if you only think you did it? What if you skipped the important part?”

And then there’s also this fear that if something did happen, my boss would say I lied about checking it, or that I was careless. Like I’d be responsible because I didn’t do it perfectly enough. That’s the part that keeps replaying in my head while I’m driving. I’m supposed to be focused, obviously, but instead I’m thinking back over the check again and again, trying to make sure I didn’t miss anything.

It’s not like I want to think about it. I know I did the check, or at least I’ll have this memory of doing it, but it doesn’t settle. The fear is basically that if I don’t get it completely right, someone could get hurt and it would be my fault, or people would think I lied about doing my job.

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it has, definitely. Mostly with work, honestly, but that kind of spills into everything else. I’ve lost rides because I couldn’t get on the road fast enough. Like someone will be waiting, or I’m supposed to be ready, and I’m still going around the cab checking things over and over, and by the time I’m done, the ride is gone or they’re upset. I’ve gotten warnings for being late or not moving when I’m supposed to, which is… humiliating, because I’m not trying to be lazy or careless. It’s actually the opposite, but it doesn’t look that way from the outside.

With colleagues, it’s awkward because people notice. They’ll be like, “Are you still checking?” or they’ll get annoyed if I’m holding things up. I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous, so I usually just say I’m making sure everything’s okay, but I can tell they think I’m overdoing it.

And friends… I barely see them now. It’s not like I don’t want to. It’s just that so much of my day gets eaten up by the checking, and then I’m behind on work, and then I’m trying to catch up afterward. So if someone asks me to hang out, I’m either late, exhausted, or I feel like I can’t because I still have things I was supposed to do.

Even my studying for my CDL keeps getting pushed back. I keep telling myself I’ll study after work, but then the day gets messed up from all the delays, and by the time I get home I’m just trying to recover or catch up. So yeah, it’s affecting a lot more than just the actual checking. It’s like everything gets backed up behind it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n5\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I can’t really get this thought to stop. Like, even when I know I did the safety check on my cab — I’ll remember doing it, or at least I think I remember doing it — this thought just hits me that maybe I missed something. Like maybe there’s something wrong with the cab and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it.\n\nAnd it’s not just a quick worry and then it goes away. It keeps coming back while I’m driving, and then I’m trying to focus on the road but in my head I’m going over it again and again, like, “Did I really check that? What if I skipped something?” It makes it hard to drive normally, and even when I’m trying to study it’s there too, like I can’t shut it up. It’s exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to perform specific actions or rituals to try and get rid of these thoughts about potentially missing something in your safety checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s kind of embarrassing because it starts as a normal safety check, like what I’m supposed to do before taking a ride, but then I can’t stop at just doing it once.\n\nI’ll go around the cab and check the tires, then the lights, then the doors, then the meter, then the paperwork, and I’ll think, “Okay, that’s done.” But then as soon as I’m about to let someone in or start driving, I get this feeling like it didn’t count, or like maybe I only looked but didn’t really check properly. So I go around again. And sometimes I’ll take pictures of things, like to prove to myself I checked them, but then even the pictures don’t always help because I’ll think maybe the picture was from before or maybe I didn’t check the right angle or something.\n\nAnd if it doesn’t feel right — like if I get interrupted, or I’m not completely sure in my head — I’ll restart the whole thing from the beginning. Tires, lights, doors, meter, paperwork, all over again. It can happen even when passengers are standing there waiting, which makes me feel terrible, and sometimes I’m already late but I still can’t just leave it. It’s like I need the check to feel “complete,” not just be complete.\n\nDoctor: Are these repeated checks you're doing intended to prevent something bad from happening, like an accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s what it feels like. Like I know the checks are supposed to prevent something bad, but I take it way past the normal amount. Before I can take a ride, I keep going around the car checking the tires, the lights, the doors, the meter, the paperwork — and then even after I’ve done all of that, I’ll think, “What if I missed one thing and someone gets hurt?”\n\nSo I do it again. And sometimes again. And if it doesn’t feel right in my head, like if I wasn’t paying attention perfectly or someone distracted me, I’ll start the whole check over from the beginning. I’ve even taken pictures to try to prove to myself I checked, but then I start doubting that too.\n\nAnd it happens even when passengers are waiting there and I’m already late, which makes me feel awful, but I still feel like I can’t just drive off unless I’m sure. It’s like I’m trying to stop an accident or stop myself from being blamed for one, even if part of me knows I already checked.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you fear might happen if you don't complete these checks perfectly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the main thing is that I’ll miss something on the cab and then somebody gets hurt because of it. Like maybe I didn’t really check a tire properly, or a light, or something with the doors, and then I drive off and something happens. Even if I remember doing the check, there’s this thought that comes in like, “But what if you only think you did it? What if you skipped the important part?”\n\nAnd then there’s also this fear that if something did happen, my boss would say I lied about checking it, or that I was careless. Like I’d be responsible because I didn’t do it perfectly enough. That’s the part that keeps replaying in my head while I’m driving. I’m supposed to be focused, obviously, but instead I’m thinking back over the check again and again, trying to make sure I didn’t miss anything.\n\nIt’s not like I want to think about it. I know I did the check, or at least I’ll have this memory of doing it, but it doesn’t settle. The fear is basically that if I don’t get it completely right, someone could get hurt and it would be my fault, or people would think I lied about doing my job.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking rituals affecting your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, definitely. Mostly with work, honestly, but that kind of spills into everything else. I’ve lost rides because I couldn’t get on the road fast enough. Like someone will be waiting, or I’m supposed to be ready, and I’m still going around the cab checking things over and over, and by the time I’m done, the ride is gone or they’re upset. I’ve gotten warnings for being late or not moving when I’m supposed to, which is… humiliating, because I’m not trying to be lazy or careless. It’s actually the opposite, but it doesn’t look that way from the outside.\n\nWith colleagues, it’s awkward because people notice. They’ll be like, “Are you still checking?” or they’ll get annoyed if I’m holding things up. I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous, so I usually just say I’m making sure everything’s okay, but I can tell they think I’m overdoing it.\n\nAnd friends… I barely see them now. It’s not like I don’t want to. It’s just that so much of my day gets eaten up by the checking, and then I’m behind on work, and then I’m trying to catch up afterward. So if someone asks me to hang out, I’m either late, exhausted, or I feel like I can’t because I still have things I was supposed to do.\n\nEven my studying for my CDL keeps getting pushed back. I keep telling myself I’ll study after work, but then the day gets messed up from all the delays, and by the time I get home I’m just trying to recover or catch up. So yeah, it’s affecting a lot more than just the actual checking. It’s like everything gets backed up behind it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I keep telling myself I’ll study after work, but then the day gets messed up from all the delays, and by the time I get home I’m just trying to recover or catch up. So yeah, it’s affecting a lot more than just the actual checking. It’s like everything gets backed up behind it.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you having these repetitive, intrusive thoughts about anything else besides the safety checks on your cab?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 19 / taxi_driver / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because even when I know I did my safety check, this thought keeps hitting me that I missed something on the cab and somebody could get hurt or my boss will say I lied, and I can't make it shut up while I'm driving or trying to study.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Mostly it’s that same cab safety thing. Like, it follows me around more than I’d expect. It’s not just when I’m standing there doing the check — it keeps coming back after I’ve already finished, even when I know I did it.

I’ll be driving and this thought just hits me like, “What if you missed something? What if a tire wasn’t right, or the door wasn’t secure, and somebody gets hurt?” And then it turns into, “What if my boss thinks I lied about checking it?” or that I said I did it but didn’t really do it properly. I keep replaying it in my head, trying to prove to myself that I did everything, but it doesn’t really shut off.

And it happens when I’m trying to study too. Like I’ll sit down and try to focus, and my mind goes right back to the cab — did I check this, did I miss that, what if something happens because of me. So… besides the safety checks, not really some whole separate topic, but the thoughts about the checks keep intruding into everything else.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I can’t really get this thought to stop. Like, even when I know I did the safety check on my cab — I’ll remember doing it, or at least I think I remember doing it — this thought just hits me that maybe I missed something. Like maybe there’s something wrong with the cab and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it.\n\nAnd it’s not just a quick worry and then it goes away. It keeps coming back while I’m driving, and then I’m trying to focus on the road but in my head I’m going over it again and again, like, “Did I really check that? What if I skipped something?” It makes it hard to drive normally, and even when I’m trying to study it’s there too, like I can’t shut it up. It’s exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to perform specific actions or rituals to try and get rid of these thoughts about potentially missing something in your safety checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s kind of embarrassing because it starts as a normal safety check, like what I’m supposed to do before taking a ride, but then I can’t stop at just doing it once.\n\nI’ll go around the cab and check the tires, then the lights, then the doors, then the meter, then the paperwork, and I’ll think, “Okay, that’s done.” But then as soon as I’m about to let someone in or start driving, I get this feeling like it didn’t count, or like maybe I only looked but didn’t really check properly. So I go around again. And sometimes I’ll take pictures of things, like to prove to myself I checked them, but then even the pictures don’t always help because I’ll think maybe the picture was from before or maybe I didn’t check the right angle or something.\n\nAnd if it doesn’t feel right — like if I get interrupted, or I’m not completely sure in my head — I’ll restart the whole thing from the beginning. Tires, lights, doors, meter, paperwork, all over again. It can happen even when passengers are standing there waiting, which makes me feel terrible, and sometimes I’m already late but I still can’t just leave it. It’s like I need the check to feel “complete,” not just be complete.\n\nDoctor: Are these repeated checks you're doing intended to prevent something bad from happening, like an accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s what it feels like. Like I know the checks are supposed to prevent something bad, but I take it way past the normal amount. Before I can take a ride, I keep going around the car checking the tires, the lights, the doors, the meter, the paperwork — and then even after I’ve done all of that, I’ll think, “What if I missed one thing and someone gets hurt?”\n\nSo I do it again. And sometimes again. And if it doesn’t feel right in my head, like if I wasn’t paying attention perfectly or someone distracted me, I’ll start the whole check over from the beginning. I’ve even taken pictures to try to prove to myself I checked, but then I start doubting that too.\n\nAnd it happens even when passengers are waiting there and I’m already late, which makes me feel awful, but I still feel like I can’t just drive off unless I’m sure. It’s like I’m trying to stop an accident or stop myself from being blamed for one, even if part of me knows I already checked.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you fear might happen if you don't complete these checks perfectly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the main thing is that I’ll miss something on the cab and then somebody gets hurt because of it. Like maybe I didn’t really check a tire properly, or a light, or something with the doors, and then I drive off and something happens. Even if I remember doing the check, there’s this thought that comes in like, “But what if you only think you did it? What if you skipped the important part?”\n\nAnd then there’s also this fear that if something did happen, my boss would say I lied about checking it, or that I was careless. Like I’d be responsible because I didn’t do it perfectly enough. That’s the part that keeps replaying in my head while I’m driving. I’m supposed to be focused, obviously, but instead I’m thinking back over the check again and again, trying to make sure I didn’t miss anything.\n\nIt’s not like I want to think about it. I know I did the check, or at least I’ll have this memory of doing it, but it doesn’t settle. The fear is basically that if I don’t get it completely right, someone could get hurt and it would be my fault, or people would think I lied about doing my job.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking rituals affecting your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, definitely. Mostly with work, honestly, but that kind of spills into everything else. I’ve lost rides because I couldn’t get on the road fast enough. Like someone will be waiting, or I’m supposed to be ready, and I’m still going around the cab checking things over and over, and by the time I’m done, the ride is gone or they’re upset. I’ve gotten warnings for being late or not moving when I’m supposed to, which is… humiliating, because I’m not trying to be lazy or careless. It’s actually the opposite, but it doesn’t look that way from the outside.\n\nWith colleagues, it’s awkward because people notice. They’ll be like, “Are you still checking?” or they’ll get annoyed if I’m holding things up. I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous, so I usually just say I’m making sure everything’s okay, but I can tell they think I’m overdoing it.\n\nAnd friends… I barely see them now. It’s not like I don’t want to. It’s just that so much of my day gets eaten up by the checking, and then I’m behind on work, and then I’m trying to catch up afterward. So if someone asks me to hang out, I’m either late, exhausted, or I feel like I can’t because I still have things I was supposed to do.\n\nEven my studying for my CDL keeps getting pushed back. I keep telling myself I’ll study after work, but then the day gets messed up from all the delays, and by the time I get home I’m just trying to recover or catch up. So yeah, it’s affecting a lot more than just the actual checking. It’s like everything gets backed up behind it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having these repetitive, intrusive thoughts about anything else besides the safety checks on your cab?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that same cab safety thing. Like, it follows me around more than I’d expect. It’s not just when I’m standing there doing the check — it keeps coming back after I’ve already finished, even when I know I did it.\n\nI’ll be driving and this thought just hits me like, “What if you missed something? What if a tire wasn’t right, or the door wasn’t secure, and somebody gets hurt?” And then it turns into, “What if my boss thinks I lied about checking it?” or that I said I did it but didn’t really do it properly. I keep replaying it in my head, trying to prove to myself that I did everything, but it doesn’t really shut off.\n\nAnd it happens when I’m trying to study too. Like I’ll sit down and try to focus, and my mind goes right back to the cab — did I check this, did I miss that, what if something happens because of me. So… besides the safety checks, not really some whole separate topic, but the thoughts about the checks keep intruding into everything else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I know I did the check, or at least I’ll have this memory of doing it, but it doesn’t settle. The fear is basically that if I don’t get it completely right, someone could get hurt and it would be my fault, or people would think I lied about doing my job.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking rituals affecting your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, definitely. Mostly with work, honestly, but that kind of spills into everything else. I’ve lost rides because I couldn’t get on the road fast enough. Like someone will be waiting, or I’m supposed to be ready, and I’m still going around the cab checking things over and over, and by the time I’m done, the ride is gone or they’re upset. I’ve gotten warnings for being late or not moving when I’m supposed to, which is… humiliating, because I’m not trying to be lazy or careless. It’s actually the opposite, but it doesn’t look that way from the outside.\n\nWith colleagues, it’s awkward because people notice. They’ll be like, “Are you still checking?” or they’ll get annoyed if I’m holding things up. I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous, so I usually just say I’m making sure everything’s okay, but I can tell they think I’m overdoing it.\n\nAnd friends… I barely see them now. It’s not like I don’t want to. It’s just that so much of my day gets eaten up by the checking, and then I’m behind on work, and then I’m trying to catch up afterward. So if someone asks me to hang out, I’m either late, exhausted, or I feel like I can’t because I still have things I was supposed to do.\n\nEven my studying for my CDL keeps getting pushed back. I keep telling myself I’ll study after work, but then the day gets messed up from all the delays, and by the time I get home I’m just trying to recover or catch up. So yeah, it’s affecting a lot more than just the actual checking. It’s like everything gets backed up behind it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having these repetitive, intrusive thoughts about anything else besides the safety checks on your cab?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that same cab safety thing. Like, it follows me around more than I’d expect. It’s not just when I’m standing there doing the check — it keeps coming back after I’ve already finished, even when I know I did it.\n\nI’ll be driving and this thought just hits me like, “What if you missed something? What if a tire wasn’t right, or the door wasn’t secure, and somebody gets hurt?” And then it turns into, “What if my boss thinks I lied about checking it?” or that I said I did it but didn’t really do it properly. I keep replaying it in my head, trying to prove to myself that I did everything, but it doesn’t really shut off.\n\nAnd it happens when I’m trying to study too. Like I’ll sit down and try to focus, and my mind goes right back to the cab — did I check this, did I miss that, what if something happens because of me. So… besides the safety checks, not really some whole separate topic, but the thoughts about the checks keep intruding into everything else.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you having these kinds of intrusive thoughts about anything else in your life besides the cab safety checks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 19 / taxi_driver / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because even when I know I did my safety check, this thought keeps hitting me that I missed something on the cab and somebody could get hurt or my boss will say I lied, and I can't make it shut up while I'm driving or trying to study.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really anything totally separate, no. It’s mainly the cab checks, but it doesn’t stay at the cab, if that makes sense. Like even when I know I did the safety check, the thought keeps coming back anyway — that I missed something, or I only think I checked it, and then somebody could get hurt because of me.

And then it kind of branches into this thing where I picture my boss saying I lied, like I said I checked it but I didn’t really do it right. That part really gets stuck in my head. I’ll be driving and trying to focus on the road, and it’s still there in the background, like, “What if you missed it? What if something happens?” Or I’ll try to study, and instead of paying attention I’m going back over the check in my mind again.

So it’s not like I have a bunch of different fears about different parts of my life. It’s mostly that one thing, but it follows me into everything and I can’t really make it shut up.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I can’t really get this thought to stop. Like, even when I know I did the safety check on my cab — I’ll remember doing it, or at least I think I remember doing it — this thought just hits me that maybe I missed something. Like maybe there’s something wrong with the cab and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it.\n\nAnd it’s not just a quick worry and then it goes away. It keeps coming back while I’m driving, and then I’m trying to focus on the road but in my head I’m going over it again and again, like, “Did I really check that? What if I skipped something?” It makes it hard to drive normally, and even when I’m trying to study it’s there too, like I can’t shut it up. It’s exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to perform specific actions or rituals to try and get rid of these thoughts about potentially missing something in your safety checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s kind of embarrassing because it starts as a normal safety check, like what I’m supposed to do before taking a ride, but then I can’t stop at just doing it once.\n\nI’ll go around the cab and check the tires, then the lights, then the doors, then the meter, then the paperwork, and I’ll think, “Okay, that’s done.” But then as soon as I’m about to let someone in or start driving, I get this feeling like it didn’t count, or like maybe I only looked but didn’t really check properly. So I go around again. And sometimes I’ll take pictures of things, like to prove to myself I checked them, but then even the pictures don’t always help because I’ll think maybe the picture was from before or maybe I didn’t check the right angle or something.\n\nAnd if it doesn’t feel right — like if I get interrupted, or I’m not completely sure in my head — I’ll restart the whole thing from the beginning. Tires, lights, doors, meter, paperwork, all over again. It can happen even when passengers are standing there waiting, which makes me feel terrible, and sometimes I’m already late but I still can’t just leave it. It’s like I need the check to feel “complete,” not just be complete.\n\nDoctor: Are these repeated checks you're doing intended to prevent something bad from happening, like an accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s what it feels like. Like I know the checks are supposed to prevent something bad, but I take it way past the normal amount. Before I can take a ride, I keep going around the car checking the tires, the lights, the doors, the meter, the paperwork — and then even after I’ve done all of that, I’ll think, “What if I missed one thing and someone gets hurt?”\n\nSo I do it again. And sometimes again. And if it doesn’t feel right in my head, like if I wasn’t paying attention perfectly or someone distracted me, I’ll start the whole check over from the beginning. I’ve even taken pictures to try to prove to myself I checked, but then I start doubting that too.\n\nAnd it happens even when passengers are waiting there and I’m already late, which makes me feel awful, but I still feel like I can’t just drive off unless I’m sure. It’s like I’m trying to stop an accident or stop myself from being blamed for one, even if part of me knows I already checked.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you fear might happen if you don't complete these checks perfectly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the main thing is that I’ll miss something on the cab and then somebody gets hurt because of it. Like maybe I didn’t really check a tire properly, or a light, or something with the doors, and then I drive off and something happens. Even if I remember doing the check, there’s this thought that comes in like, “But what if you only think you did it? What if you skipped the important part?”\n\nAnd then there’s also this fear that if something did happen, my boss would say I lied about checking it, or that I was careless. Like I’d be responsible because I didn’t do it perfectly enough. That’s the part that keeps replaying in my head while I’m driving. I’m supposed to be focused, obviously, but instead I’m thinking back over the check again and again, trying to make sure I didn’t miss anything.\n\nIt’s not like I want to think about it. I know I did the check, or at least I’ll have this memory of doing it, but it doesn’t settle. The fear is basically that if I don’t get it completely right, someone could get hurt and it would be my fault, or people would think I lied about doing my job.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking rituals affecting your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, definitely. Mostly with work, honestly, but that kind of spills into everything else. I’ve lost rides because I couldn’t get on the road fast enough. Like someone will be waiting, or I’m supposed to be ready, and I’m still going around the cab checking things over and over, and by the time I’m done, the ride is gone or they’re upset. I’ve gotten warnings for being late or not moving when I’m supposed to, which is… humiliating, because I’m not trying to be lazy or careless. It’s actually the opposite, but it doesn’t look that way from the outside.\n\nWith colleagues, it’s awkward because people notice. They’ll be like, “Are you still checking?” or they’ll get annoyed if I’m holding things up. I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous, so I usually just say I’m making sure everything’s okay, but I can tell they think I’m overdoing it.\n\nAnd friends… I barely see them now. It’s not like I don’t want to. It’s just that so much of my day gets eaten up by the checking, and then I’m behind on work, and then I’m trying to catch up afterward. So if someone asks me to hang out, I’m either late, exhausted, or I feel like I can’t because I still have things I was supposed to do.\n\nEven my studying for my CDL keeps getting pushed back. I keep telling myself I’ll study after work, but then the day gets messed up from all the delays, and by the time I get home I’m just trying to recover or catch up. So yeah, it’s affecting a lot more than just the actual checking. It’s like everything gets backed up behind it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having these repetitive, intrusive thoughts about anything else besides the safety checks on your cab?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that same cab safety thing. Like, it follows me around more than I’d expect. It’s not just when I’m standing there doing the check — it keeps coming back after I’ve already finished, even when I know I did it.\n\nI’ll be driving and this thought just hits me like, “What if you missed something? What if a tire wasn’t right, or the door wasn’t secure, and somebody gets hurt?” And then it turns into, “What if my boss thinks I lied about checking it?” or that I said I did it but didn’t really do it properly. I keep replaying it in my head, trying to prove to myself that I did everything, but it doesn’t really shut off.\n\nAnd it happens when I’m trying to study too. Like I’ll sit down and try to focus, and my mind goes right back to the cab — did I check this, did I miss that, what if something happens because of me. So… besides the safety checks, not really some whole separate topic, but the thoughts about the checks keep intruding into everything else.\n\nDoctor: Are you having these kinds of intrusive thoughts about anything else in your life besides the cab safety checks?\n\nPatient: Not really anything totally separate, no. It’s mainly the cab checks, but it doesn’t stay at the cab, if that makes sense. Like even when I know I did the safety check, the thought keeps coming back anyway — that I missed something, or I only think I checked it, and then somebody could get hurt because of me.\n\nAnd then it kind of branches into this thing where I picture my boss saying I lied, like I said I checked it but I didn’t really do it right. What if something happens?” Or I’ll try to study, and instead of paying attention I’m going back over the check in my mind again.\n\nSo it’s not like I have a bunch of different fears about different parts of my life. What if a tire wasn’t right, or the door wasn’t secure, and somebody gets hurt?” And then it turns into, “What if my boss thinks I lied about checking it?” or that I said I did it but didn’t really do it properly. I keep replaying it in my head, trying to prove to myself that I did everything, but it doesn’t really shut off.\n\nAnd it happens when I’m trying to study too. Like I’ll sit down and try to focus, and my mind goes right back to the cab — did I check this, did I miss that, what if something happens because of me. So… besides the safety checks, not really some whole separate topic, but the thoughts about the checks keep intruding into everything else.\n\nDoctor: Are you having these kinds of intrusive thoughts about anything else in your life besides the cab safety checks?\n\nPatient: Not really anything totally separate, no. It’s mainly the cab checks, but it doesn’t stay at the cab, if that makes sense. Like even when I know I did the safety check, the thought keeps coming back anyway — that I missed something, or I only think I checked it, and then somebody could get hurt because of me.\n\nAnd then it kind of branches into this thing where I picture my boss saying I lied, like I said I checked it but I didn’t really do it right. That part really gets stuck in my head. I’ll be driving and trying to focus on the road, and it’s still there in the background, like, “What if you missed it? What if something happens?” Or I’ll try to study, and instead of paying attention I’m going back over the check in my mind again.\n\nSo it’s not like I have a bunch of different fears about different parts of my life. It’s mostly that one thing, but it follows me into everything and I can’t really make it shut up.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre your intrusive thoughts more about preventing something bad from happening, or are they also about what others might think of you if something goes wrong?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s both, honestly. The biggest part is definitely trying to stop something bad from happening. Like, even after I know I did the safety check, my mind keeps hitting me with, “What if you missed something on the cab?” And then it goes straight to somebody getting hurt because I didn’t catch it. That’s the part that scares me the most.

But it also turns into what people would think, especially my boss. I get stuck on this idea that if something went wrong, he’d say I lied — like I claimed I checked it but didn’t really do it right. And I know that sounds kind of specific, but it just repeats in my head. It’s not like I can think, “Okay, I checked it, I’m done.” It keeps coming back while I’m driving, or when I’m trying to study, and I’ll be sitting there trying to focus but my brain is still back at the cab, replaying whether I missed something.

So yeah… it’s mainly about preventing someone from getting hurt, but then right behind that is this fear that people will think I was careless or dishonest. I can’t really make either part shut up.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I can’t really get this thought to stop. Like, even when I know I did the safety check on my cab — I’ll remember doing it, or at least I think I remember doing it — this thought just hits me that maybe I missed something. Like maybe there’s something wrong with the cab and someone could get hurt, or my boss will think I lied about checking it.\n\nAnd it’s not just a quick worry and then it goes away. It keeps coming back while I’m driving, and then I’m trying to focus on the road but in my head I’m going over it again and again, like, “Did I really check that? What if I skipped something?” It makes it hard to drive normally, and even when I’m trying to study it’s there too, like I can’t shut it up. It’s exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to perform specific actions or rituals to try and get rid of these thoughts about potentially missing something in your safety checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s kind of embarrassing because it starts as a normal safety check, like what I’m supposed to do before taking a ride, but then I can’t stop at just doing it once.\n\nI’ll go around the cab and check the tires, then the lights, then the doors, then the meter, then the paperwork, and I’ll think, “Okay, that’s done.” But then as soon as I’m about to let someone in or start driving, I get this feeling like it didn’t count, or like maybe I only looked but didn’t really check properly. So I go around again. And sometimes I’ll take pictures of things, like to prove to myself I checked them, but then even the pictures don’t always help because I’ll think maybe the picture was from before or maybe I didn’t check the right angle or something.\n\nAnd if it doesn’t feel right — like if I get interrupted, or I’m not completely sure in my head — I’ll restart the whole thing from the beginning. Tires, lights, doors, meter, paperwork, all over again. It can happen even when passengers are standing there waiting, which makes me feel terrible, and sometimes I’m already late but I still can’t just leave it. It’s like I need the check to feel “complete,” not just be complete.\n\nDoctor: Are these repeated checks you're doing intended to prevent something bad from happening, like an accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s what it feels like. Like I know the checks are supposed to prevent something bad, but I take it way past the normal amount. Before I can take a ride, I keep going around the car checking the tires, the lights, the doors, the meter, the paperwork — and then even after I’ve done all of that, I’ll think, “What if I missed one thing and someone gets hurt?”\n\nSo I do it again. And sometimes again. And if it doesn’t feel right in my head, like if I wasn’t paying attention perfectly or someone distracted me, I’ll start the whole check over from the beginning. I’ve even taken pictures to try to prove to myself I checked, but then I start doubting that too.\n\nAnd it happens even when passengers are waiting there and I’m already late, which makes me feel awful, but I still feel like I can’t just drive off unless I’m sure. It’s like I’m trying to stop an accident or stop myself from being blamed for one, even if part of me knows I already checked.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you fear might happen if you don't complete these checks perfectly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the main thing is that I’ll miss something on the cab and then somebody gets hurt because of it. Like maybe I didn’t really check a tire properly, or a light, or something with the doors, and then I drive off and something happens. Even if I remember doing the check, there’s this thought that comes in like, “But what if you only think you did it? What if you skipped the important part?”\n\nAnd then there’s also this fear that if something did happen, my boss would say I lied about checking it, or that I was careless. Like I’d be responsible because I didn’t do it perfectly enough. That’s the part that keeps replaying in my head while I’m driving. I’m supposed to be focused, obviously, but instead I’m thinking back over the check again and again, trying to make sure I didn’t miss anything.\n\nIt’s not like I want to think about it. I know I did the check, or at least I’ll have this memory of doing it, but it doesn’t settle. The fear is basically that if I don’t get it completely right, someone could get hurt and it would be my fault, or people would think I lied about doing my job.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking rituals affecting your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, definitely. Mostly with work, honestly, but that kind of spills into everything else. I’ve lost rides because I couldn’t get on the road fast enough. Like someone will be waiting, or I’m supposed to be ready, and I’m still going around the cab checking things over and over, and by the time I’m done, the ride is gone or they’re upset. I’ve gotten warnings for being late or not moving when I’m supposed to, which is… humiliating, because I’m not trying to be lazy or careless. It’s actually the opposite, but it doesn’t look that way from the outside.\n\nWith colleagues, it’s awkward because people notice. They’ll be like, “Are you still checking?” or they’ll get annoyed if I’m holding things up. I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous, so I usually just say I’m making sure everything’s okay, but I can tell they think I’m overdoing it.\n\nAnd friends… I barely see them now. It’s not like I don’t want to. It’s just that so much of my day gets eaten up by the checking, and then I’m behind on work, and then I’m trying to catch up afterward. So if someone asks me to hang out, I’m either late, exhausted, or I feel like I can’t because I still have things I was supposed to do.\n\nEven my studying for my CDL keeps getting pushed back. I keep telling myself I’ll study after work, but then the day gets messed up from all the delays, and by the time I get home I’m just trying to recover or catch up. So yeah, it’s affecting a lot more than just the actual checking. It’s like everything gets backed up behind it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having these repetitive, intrusive thoughts about anything else besides the safety checks on your cab?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that same cab safety thing. Like, it follows me around more than I’d expect. It’s not just when I’m standing there doing the check — it keeps coming back after I’ve already finished, even when I know I did it.\n\nI’ll be driving and this thought just hits me like, “What if you missed something? What if a tire wasn’t right, or the door wasn’t secure, and somebody gets hurt?” And then it turns into, “What if my boss thinks I lied about checking it?” or that I said I did it but didn’t really do it properly. It’s mainly the cab checks, but it doesn’t stay at the cab, if that makes sense. That part really gets stuck in my head. I’ll be driving and trying to focus on the road, and it’s still there in the background, like, “What if you missed it? What if something happens?” Or I’ll try to study, and instead of paying attention I’m going back over the check in my mind again.\n\nSo it’s not like I have a bunch of different fears about different parts of my life. It’s mostly that one thing, but it follows me into everything and I can’t really make it shut up.\n\nDoctor: Are your intrusive thoughts more about preventing something bad from happening, or are they also about what others might think of you if something goes wrong?\n\nPatient: It’s both, honestly. It’s not like I can think, “Okay, I checked it, I’m done.” It keeps coming back while I’m driving, or when I’m trying to study, and I’ll be sitting there trying to focus but my brain is still back at the cab, replaying whether I missed something.\n\nSo yeah… it’s mainly about preventing someone from getting hurt, but then right behind that is this fear that people will think I was careless or dishonest. I can’t really make either part shut up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s mostly that one thing, but it follows me into everything and I can’t really make it shut up.\n\nDoctor: Are your intrusive thoughts more about preventing something bad from happening, or are they also about what others might think of you if something goes wrong?\n\nPatient: It’s both, honestly. The biggest part is definitely trying to stop something bad from happening. I can’t really make either part shut up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. They feel like they’re coming from my own head, I guess… not like a voice outside me or like somebody’s putting them there. It’s more like my own thoughts turn against me, if that makes sense.

It’s exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the need to perform specific actions or rituals to try and get rid of these thoughts about potentially missing something in your safety checks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s kind of embarrassing because it starts as a normal safety check, like what I’m supposed to do before taking a ride, but then I can’t stop at just doing it once.\n\nI’ll go around the cab and check the tires, then the lights, then the doors, then the meter, then the paperwork, and I’ll think, “Okay, that’s done.” But then as soon as I’m about to let someone in or start driving, I get this feeling like it didn’t count, or like maybe I only looked but didn’t really check properly. So I go around again. And sometimes I’ll take pictures of things, like to prove to myself I checked them, but then even the pictures don’t always help because I’ll think maybe the picture was from before or maybe I didn’t check the right angle or something.\n\nAnd if it doesn’t feel right — like if I get interrupted, or I’m not completely sure in my head — I’ll restart the whole thing from the beginning. Tires, lights, doors, meter, paperwork, all over again. It can happen even when passengers are standing there waiting, which makes me feel terrible, and sometimes I’m already late but I still can’t just leave it. It’s like I need the check to feel “complete,” not just be complete.\n\nDoctor: Are these repeated checks you're doing intended to prevent something bad from happening, like an accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s what it feels like. Like I know the checks are supposed to prevent something bad, but I take it way past the normal amount. Before I can take a ride, I keep going around the car checking the tires, the lights, the doors, the meter, the paperwork — and then even after I’ve done all of that, I’ll think, “What if I missed one thing and someone gets hurt?”\n\nSo I do it again. And sometimes again. And if it doesn’t feel right in my head, like if I wasn’t paying attention perfectly or someone distracted me, I’ll start the whole check over from the beginning. I’ve even taken pictures to try to prove to myself I checked, but then I start doubting that too.\n\nAnd it happens even when passengers are waiting there and I’m already late, which makes me feel awful, but I still feel like I can’t just drive off unless I’m sure. It’s like I’m trying to stop an accident or stop myself from being blamed for one, even if part of me knows I already checked.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that you fear might happen if you don't complete these checks perfectly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… the main thing is that I’ll miss something on the cab and then somebody gets hurt because of it. Like maybe I didn’t really check a tire properly, or a light, or something with the doors, and then I drive off and something happens. Even if I remember doing the check, there’s this thought that comes in like, “But what if you only think you did it? What if you skipped the important part?”\n\nAnd then there’s also this fear that if something did happen, my boss would say I lied about checking it, or that I was careless. Like I’d be responsible because I didn’t do it perfectly enough. That’s the part that keeps replaying in my head while I’m driving. I’m supposed to be focused, obviously, but instead I’m thinking back over the check again and again, trying to make sure I didn’t miss anything.\n\nIt’s not like I want to think about it. I know I did the check, or at least I’ll have this memory of doing it, but it doesn’t settle. The fear is basically that if I don’t get it completely right, someone could get hurt and it would be my fault, or people would think I lied about doing my job.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and checking rituals affecting your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, definitely. Mostly with work, honestly, but that kind of spills into everything else. I’ve lost rides because I couldn’t get on the road fast enough. Like someone will be waiting, or I’m supposed to be ready, and I’m still going around the cab checking things over and over, and by the time I’m done, the ride is gone or they’re upset. I’ve gotten warnings for being late or not moving when I’m supposed to, which is… humiliating, because I’m not trying to be lazy or careless. It’s actually the opposite, but it doesn’t look that way from the outside.\n\nWith colleagues, it’s awkward because people notice. They’ll be like, “Are you still checking?” or they’ll get annoyed if I’m holding things up. I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous, so I usually just say I’m making sure everything’s okay, but I can tell they think I’m overdoing it.\n\nAnd friends… I barely see them now. It’s not like I don’t want to. It’s just that so much of my day gets eaten up by the checking, and then I’m behind on work, and then I’m trying to catch up afterward. So if someone asks me to hang out, I’m either late, exhausted, or I feel like I can’t because I still have things I was supposed to do.\n\nEven my studying for my CDL keeps getting pushed back. I keep telling myself I’ll study after work, but then the day gets messed up from all the delays, and by the time I get home I’m just trying to recover or catch up. So yeah, it’s affecting a lot more than just the actual checking. It’s like everything gets backed up behind it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having these repetitive, intrusive thoughts about anything else besides the safety checks on your cab?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that same cab safety thing. Like, it follows me around more than I’d expect. It’s not just when I’m standing there doing the check — it keeps coming back after I’ve already finished, even when I know I did it.\n\nI’ll be driving and this thought just hits me like, “What if you missed something? What if a tire wasn’t right, or the door wasn’t secure, and somebody gets hurt?” And then it turns into, “What if my boss thinks I lied about checking it?” or that I said I did it but didn’t really do it properly. I keep replaying it in my head, trying to prove to myself that I did everything, but it doesn’t really shut off.\n\nAnd it happens when I’m trying to study too. Like I’ll sit down and try to focus, and my mind goes right back to the cab — did I check this, did I miss that, what if something happens because of me. So… besides the safety checks, not really some whole separate topic, but the thoughts about the checks keep intruding into everything else.\n\nDoctor: Are you having these kinds of intrusive thoughts about anything else in your life besides the cab safety checks?\n\nPatient: Not really anything totally separate, no. It’s mainly the cab checks, but it doesn’t stay at the cab, if that makes sense. Like even when I know I did the safety check, the thought keeps coming back anyway — that I missed something, or I only think I checked it, and then somebody could get hurt because of me.\n\nAnd then it kind of branches into this thing where I picture my boss saying I lied, like I said I checked it but I didn’t really do it right. That part really gets stuck in my head. I’ll be driving and trying to focus on the road, and it’s still there in the background, like, “What if you missed it? What if something happens?” Or I’ll try to study, and instead of paying attention I’m going back over the check in my mind again.\n\nSo it’s not like I have a bunch of different fears about different parts of my life. It’s mostly that one thing, but it follows me into everything and I can’t really make it shut up.\n\nDoctor: Are your intrusive thoughts more about preventing something bad from happening, or are they also about what others might think of you if something goes wrong?\n\nPatient: It’s both, honestly. The biggest part is definitely trying to stop something bad from happening. Like, even after I know I did the safety check, my mind keeps hitting me with, “What if you missed something on the cab?” And then it goes straight to somebody getting hurt because I didn’t catch it. That’s the part that scares me the most.\n\nBut it also turns into what people would think, especially my boss. I get stuck on this idea that if something went wrong, he’d say I lied — like I claimed I checked it but didn’t really do it right. And I know that sounds kind of specific, but it just repeats in my head. It’s not like I can think, “Okay, I checked it, I’m done.” It keeps coming back while I’m driving, or when I’m trying to study, and I’ll be sitting there trying to focus but my brain is still back at the cab, replaying whether I missed something.\n\nSo yeah… it’s mainly about preventing someone from getting hurt, but then right behind that is this fear that people will think I was careless or dishonest. I can’t really make either part shut up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing something in your safety checks are a part of you, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from my own head, I guess… not like a voice outside me or like somebody’s putting them there. It’s more like my own thoughts turn against me, if that makes sense.\n\nLike I’ll know I did the safety check. I can even remember doing it. But then this thought keeps hitting me anyway, like, “What if you missed something on the cab?” And it doesn’t feel like I chose to think that. It just pops back in, over and over, and then I’m stuck thinking somebody could get hurt or my boss could say I lied about checking it.\n\nSo it’s part of my thoughts, but it doesn’t feel like the normal kind of thinking where I can just decide to stop. It’s in my own mind, but it feels unwanted and it won’t shut up, especially when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

No, I don’t want to think about them at all. That’s kind of the whole problem. They’ll be like, “Are you still checking?” or they’ll get annoyed if I’m holding things up. I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding ridiculous, so I usually just say I’m making sure everything’s okay, but I can tell they think I’m overdoing it.\n\nAnd friends… I barely see them now. It’s not like I don’t want to. It’s just that so much of my day gets eaten up by the checking, and then I’m behind on work, and then I’m trying to catch up afterward. So if someone asks me to hang out, I’m either late, exhausted, or I feel like I can’t because I still have things I was supposed to do.\n\nEven my studying for my CDL keeps getting pushed back. I keep telling myself I’ll study after work, but then the day gets messed up from all the delays, and by the time I get home I’m just trying to recover or catch up. So yeah, it’s affecting a lot more than just the actual checking. It’s like everything gets backed up behind it.\n\nDoctor: Are you having these repetitive, intrusive thoughts about anything else besides the safety checks on your cab?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that same cab safety thing. Like, it follows me around more than I’d expect. It’s not just when I’m standing there doing the check — it keeps coming back after I’ve already finished, even when I know I did it.\n\nI’ll be driving and this thought just hits me like, “What if you missed something? What if a tire wasn’t right, or the door wasn’t secure, and somebody gets hurt?” And then it turns into, “What if my boss thinks I lied about checking it?” or that I said I did it but didn’t really do it properly. I keep replaying it in my head, trying to prove to myself that I did everything, but it doesn’t really shut off.\n\nAnd it happens when I’m trying to study too. Like I’ll sit down and try to focus, and my mind goes right back to the cab — did I check this, did I miss that, what if something happens because of me. So… besides the safety checks, not really some whole separate topic, but the thoughts about the checks keep intruding into everything else.\n\nDoctor: Are you having these kinds of intrusive thoughts about anything else in your life besides the cab safety checks?\n\nPatient: Not really anything totally separate, no. It’s mainly the cab checks, but it doesn’t stay at the cab, if that makes sense. Like even when I know I did the safety check, the thought keeps coming back anyway — that I missed something, or I only think I checked it, and then somebody could get hurt because of me.\n\nAnd then it kind of branches into this thing where I picture my boss saying I lied, like I said I checked it but I didn’t really do it right. That part really gets stuck in my head. I’ll be driving and trying to focus on the road, and it’s still there in the background, like, “What if you missed it? What if something happens?” Or I’ll try to study, and instead of paying attention I’m going back over the check in my mind again.\n\nSo it’s not like I have a bunch of different fears about different parts of my life. It’s mostly that one thing, but it follows me into everything and I can’t really make it shut up.\n\nDoctor: Are your intrusive thoughts more about preventing something bad from happening, or are they also about what others might think of you if something goes wrong?\n\nPatient: It’s both, honestly. The biggest part is definitely trying to stop something bad from happening. Like, even after I know I did the safety check, my mind keeps hitting me with, “What if you missed something on the cab?” And then it goes straight to somebody getting hurt because I didn’t catch it. That’s the part that scares me the most.\n\nBut it also turns into what people would think, especially my boss. I get stuck on this idea that if something went wrong, he’d say I lied — like I claimed I checked it but didn’t really do it right. And I know that sounds kind of specific, but it just repeats in my head. It’s not like I can think, “Okay, I checked it, I’m done.” It keeps coming back while I’m driving, or when I’m trying to study, and I’ll be sitting there trying to focus but my brain is still back at the cab, replaying whether I missed something.\n\nSo yeah… it’s mainly about preventing someone from getting hurt, but then right behind that is this fear that people will think I was careless or dishonest. I can’t really make either part shut up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing something in your safety checks are a part of you, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: They feel like they’re coming from my own head, I guess… not like a voice outside me or like somebody’s putting them there. It’s more like my own thoughts turn against me, if that makes sense.\n\nLike I’ll know I did the safety check. I can even remember doing it. But then this thought keeps hitting me anyway, like, “What if you missed something on the cab?” And it doesn’t feel like I chose to think that. It just pops back in, over and over, and then I’m stuck thinking somebody could get hurt or my boss could say I lied about checking it.\n\nSo it’s part of my thoughts, but it doesn’t feel like the normal kind of thinking where I can just decide to stop. It’s in my own mind, but it feels unwanted and it won’t shut up, especially when I’m driving or trying to study.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about missing something in your safety checks are something you want to think about, or do they feel unwanted?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t want to think about them at all. That’s kind of the whole problem. I’ll do the safety check and I’ll know, like logically, that I did it, but then the thought just comes right back anyway — like, what if I missed something on the cab, what if there’s something wrong and somebody gets hurt because of me.\n\nIt feels really unwanted, like it keeps forcing itself back into my head. I’m not sitting there trying to worry about it or analyze it on purpose. I’ll be driving and I need to be paying attention, or I’ll be trying to study, and it’s still there, replaying the check or making me doubt whether I really did it right. And then it turns into, what if my boss thinks I lied and says I didn’t actually check it properly.\n\nSo yeah, it’s definitely not something I want. I’ve tried to just tell myself, “You checked it, stop thinking about it,” but it doesn’t really shut up.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I’ve tried to just tell myself, “You checked it, stop thinking about it,” but it doesn’t really shut up.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow distressed do you feel when these unwanted thoughts about missing something in your safety checks occur?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 19 / taxi_driver / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because even when I know I did my safety check, this thought keeps hitting me that I missed something on the cab and somebody could get hurt or my boss will say I lied, and I can't make it shut up while I'm driving or trying to study.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Really distressed. Like… it doesn’t just feel like a normal worry where I can go, “Okay, I checked it, move on.” It hits me hard because the thought is always that I missed something on the cab and somebody could get hurt because of me. And even if part of me knows I did the safety check, the feeling doesn’t match that. It’s like my chest tightens and my brain just keeps circling back to it.

And then I start thinking, what if my boss says I lied, like I said I checked it but I didn’t really do it right. That part makes it worse, because then it’s not just someone getting hurt, it’s also me being seen as careless or dishonest. I can’t really calm down from it easily.

It happens while I’m driving, which is awful because I’m supposed to be focused, and it happens when I’m trying to study too. I’ll be sitting there trying to read something and I’m still mentally back at the cab, replaying the safety check. So yeah, it’s very distressing. It feels like I can’t make it shut up, even when I know I already checked.
